# facts.md — what is true about learned models in factories and vehicles, and the ways into them

Merged 2026-10-17 by the main model from 17 research files (11 planned angles plus 6 gap fills chosen by a
completeness critic). Every row was checked by an independent fact checker who re-opened the cited source
(verdicts in `verify-<name>.md`). Ids are the original ids. This file holds a compact table of every row
(statement, source, date, reliability, tags). The exact quotes and the checker's notes stay in the
per-topic files `facts-<name>.md`; a section writer who needs a quote reads the row there.

| part | file | ids | what it covers |
|---|---|---|---|
| A mechanism | facts-A-arm.md | A1–A60 | learned policies / VLAs on robot arms; Denso b-CAP path; safety functions |
| A mechanism | facts-A-amr.md | A101–A160 | AMR/AGV localisation, VDA 5050, safety scanners, learned perception |
| A mechanism | facts-A-car.md | A201–A256 | ADAS perception in tier-1 parts, command path, OTA, fleet data loops |
| A mechanism | facts-A-inspect.md | A301–A359 | AI visual inspection stations and AI process control in parts plants |
| A mechanism | facts-A-attack.md | A401–A458 | the attack methods as mechanisms on a learned model |
| A mechanism | facts-A-supply.md | A501–A560 | the model and data supply chain, file formats, signing, OTA |
| B scale | facts-B-attack.md | B1–B55 | success rates, costs, poison fractions, incidents, advisory counts |
| B scale | facts-B-industry.md | B101–B162 | Bosch/Denso size, robot numbers, model sizes, rates, legal dates |
| C boundaries | facts-C-controls.md | C1–C60 | what standards and existing controls cover and leave open |
| C boundaries | facts-C-myths.md | C101–C162 | what the simple story gets wrong; what is deployed now |
| C boundaries | facts-C-supplier.md | C201–C260 | Bosch/Denso/peer PSIRTs, advisories, incidents, own AI-security work |
| gap fill | facts-G1.md | G1–G36 | what OT IDS, safety scanners and safety PLCs actually observe |
| gap fill | facts-G2.md | G101–G131 | SecOC/E2E, secure boot and model files, UN R156 and model updates |
| gap fill | facts-G3.md | G201–G248 | change management, incoming inspection, standards clause status, EU law |
| gap fill | facts-G4.md | G301–G334 | model-host servers, edge MLOps, instruction channels, prompt injection |
| gap fill | facts-G5.md | G401–G426 | published attacks on AMR localisation and on AI inspection |
| gap fill | facts-G6.md | G501–G528 | repaired headline numbers (phantoms, McAfee), Denso rows, Annex I numbering |

Conventions: reliability primary / secondary / hearsay / derived (arithmetic on primary rows) / unverified.
Tags: [factory] lands on the factory floor; [product] lands in the shipped product; [supply-chain] lands in
the model and data supply chain; [general]. "corrected" means the checker changed the row.

## What we do not know

1. **Whether any of these attacks has happened in the wild against a vehicle, robot or inspection station.** No source
   reports one (C109, C35, A-attack "could not find", G5 "could not find", C-supplier "could not find"). Only attacks on
   model files and ML frameworks are reported in the wild (A445, A446, A451, A452). Absence in public sources is not
   proof of absence.
2. **Whether Bosch's or Denso's production models are vulnerable to the published attacks.** Every published attack
   targets open-source stacks, research sensors or older shipped products (C119, B10, G514); none targets a Bosch or
   Denso part (C-supplier "could not find").
3. **Factory AMRs and inspection stations specifically.** No published attack on a factory AMR's 2D lidar or safety
   scanner (G426), and no false-pass demonstration on a real inspection station (G421–G423, G5).
4. **How Bosch and Denso protect their own commands and model files.** Whether ADAS-to-actuator messages use SecOC
   (G2), whether DNN binaries are inside secure boot on the SoCs they use (G111, G2), and their ASIL/monitor design
   (A-car) are not public.
5. **Whether a retrain or a new weights file counts as a change** under PPAP, ISO 26262-8, MoC or IEC 62443-2-3. No
   public text says so (G231–G235, G3).
6. **Clause text of ISO/SAE 21434, ISO/PAS 8800, ISO 10218:2025, IEC 62443** on learned models (paywalled; only
   scopes read: G219–G228).
7. **Whether replacing neural-network weights is a "software update" under UN R156.** No regulator text says it in
   words (G119–G124); the definitions ("digital data and instruction") suggest it is.
8. **What the Machinery Regulation's AI delegated acts (due to apply by 2 Aug 2028) will require**, and which
   harmonised standards will exist (G204, G217).
9. **Denso's own statements.** denso.com was unreadable to every agent; Denso rows are mostly secondary (C227–C229,
   A212, A27–A28).
10. **Likelihood and cost of each attack** — out of scope by the brief.

## Bearing on the question (the closing rests on these)

- **Ways in through the scene need no network access** and are invisible to network controls: C110 (Keen Lab: no root
  needed), G501 (phantoms), A404, A419–A421, G401; OT IDS lists decode none of b-CAP, ROS, VDA 5050 and have no
  model asset class (G6, G12, G15); firewalls and IDS observe traffic (C48, C49).
- **Ways in through training data change the weights without changing anything at the plant**, and integrity
  controls cannot tell: backdoors pass clean tests (A426, C52); a signature proves "unchanged since signed", not
  behaviour (A554, C51); secure boot on DRIVE OS has no DNN category (G111); TensorRT validity is not a tamper check and
  weights can be refitted (G114, G116); scanning is not a poisoning check (C148); robustness to supply-chain poisoning
  is "a critical open problem" (C32). For a supplier the training data is shared and crosses boundaries: ~50 plants on
  one software (A342), central labels across sites (A327), shared foundation model (A331), labelling vendors (A518,
  A519), OEM-supplied models in a Denso-made unit (A213, A215), OTA to cars (A252, A226).
- **Instructions are input numbers in the same message as the images** (G302, A2), so text in the scene is an
  instruction channel (A437, C145); prompt injection "may never be totally mitigated" and needs deterministic limits
  outside the model (C133, C131, C40). Denso's generative-AI robot takes spoken instructions (A27, C245; pilot).
- **The command path after the model is where existing controls work** — and where the old attacks still hit:
  b-CAP over TCP with no check code and rights by path (A37, A38), VDA 5050 security out of scope (A104, A105, G322),
  CAN with no source authentication (A237), NEXO torque changed silently (B35), MiR safety PLC default password
  (A138, G413). SecOC checks authenticity and freshness, not correctness (G101–G103).
- **Safety functions bound motion, not meaning:** axis, speed, standstill, force (A51, A49, C124); law defines safety
  by injury risk (C126); the Machinery Regulation's "defined task and movement space" for self-evolving systems (C23)
  is a bound a non-learned check can enforce.
- **Standards:** R155 has zero ML terms (C8, C255) but obliges the OEM to consider "any other relevant risk" (C9) and
  to manage supplier risks (C251–C253); the AI Act names poisoning, model poisoning and adversarial examples (C14,
  G246) but reaches robots and cars only through sector law (C16–C20, G204, G205).
- **Now vs near future:** learned perception ships in ADAS parts today (A201, A207, A212, A213, C151) and AI
  inspection runs in about 50 Bosch plants (A342); VLA arms are pilots (A-arm production list, A27, C154, C157).

| id | statement | source | date | reliability | tags |

| A101 | VDA 5050 is at version 3.0.0, published by the VDA (with VDMA) in March 2026. | https://vda.de/en/topics/automotive-industry/vda-5050 (version line); https://github.com/VDA5050/VDA5050/releases (release 3.0.0); cover page of VDA5050_EN.md | 2026-03-19 | primary | general  |
| A102 | VDA 5050 transport is MQTT (minimum 3.1.1) carrying JSON; order, instantActions, state, factsheet, zoneSet, responses and visualization use QoS 0, connection uses QoS 1. | VDA5050_EN.md §4 and §4.1 (https://github.com/VDA5050/VDA5050) | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A103 | VDA 5050 defines eight topics under the pattern interfaceName/majorVersion/manufacturer/serialNumber/topic; fleet control publishes order, instantActions, zoneSet and responses; the robot publishes state, visualization, connection and factsheet. | VDA5050_EN.md §4.2 and §4.3 Table 2 | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A104 | VDA 5050 does not specify cybersecurity: it leaves protocol security to broker configuration and lists cybersecurity measures as out of scope. | VDA5050_EN.md §2 (Scope, last bullet) and §4.1 (last sentence) | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A105 | Apart from the §2 scope exclusion and the §4.1 sentence that leaves protocol security to broker configuration, the only security text in VDA 5050 is the certificate-update action, which says robot communication should be secured, that MQTT is "typically" secured with TLS and that the sender of an instantAction "cannot be verified"; the words password, authentication, signature and integrity do not occur in the document. | VDA5050_EN.md §6.2.3.3 "Update mobile robot certificate"; grep of the whole file | 2026-03 | primary | factory corrected |
| A106 | VDA 5050 states that it is not a safety standard and defines no safety requirements. | VDA5050_EN.md §2 (Scope) | 2026-03 | primary | general  |
| A107 | A VDA 5050 order carries a header (headerId, timestamp, version, manufacturer, serialNumber), an orderId, an orderUpdateId, and arrays of nodes and edges; nodes carry nodePosition (x, y, theta, allowedDeviationXY, mapId) and actions; edges carry maximumSpeed, optional NURBS trajectory, optional corridor and actions. | VDA5050_EN.md §7.2 and §7.3 | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A108 | A VDA 5050 order is split into a released "base" the robot must drive and an unreleased "horizon" it must not drive; the robot stops at the last base node (decision point) unless the fleet control extends the base, and a sent base cannot be changed. | VDA5050_EN.md §6.1.1 and §6.1.2 | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A109 | In VDA 5050, a freely navigating robot plans its own trajectory but "shall follow" a trajectory that the order contains, and an edge can carry a corridor within which the robot may deviate to avoid obstacles; such robots publish their planned and intermediate paths in the state. | VDA5050_EN.md §3.7, §6.1.5 / §7.3 (corridor), §6.8 | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A110 | A VDA 5050 state message is sent on events or "at least every 30 seconds" and carries the pose (x, y, theta, mapId, localized, optional localizationScore), driving, operatingMode, errors and a safetyState with activeEmergencyStop and fieldViolation. | VDA5050_EN.md §6.6 and §7.8 (mobileRobotPosition, safetyState tables) | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A111 | VDA 5050 leaves the rate of the `visualization` topic to the integrator and moves minimum order and state intervals into the robot's factsheet (protocolLimits.timing). | VDA5050_EN.md §6.7; §7.10 protocolLimits table (minimumOrderInterval, minimumStateInterval, defaultStateInterval, visualizationInterval) | 2026-03 | primary | general  |
| A112 | VDA 5050 instant actions include cancelOrder, startPause and stopPause (mandatory on every robot), plus initializePosition (overrides the pose), downloadMap, enableMap, deleteMap, downloadZoneSet, updateCertificate, startHibernation and shutdown. | VDA5050_EN.md §6.2.3 and §6.2.3.1 Table 4 | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A113 | VDA 5050 distributes maps by pull: the fleet control sends a downloadMap instant action with a URL, the robot fetches the file from a map server, and the hash parameter is optional; the fleet control is responsible for enabling the correct map. | VDA5050_EN.md §6.3.1, §6.3.3 and Table 4 (downloadMap, downloadZoneSet) | 2026-03 | primary | factory supply-chain  |
| A114 | VDA 5050 zones let the fleet control block areas (BLOCKED), gate them (RELEASE) and cap speed (SPEED_LIMIT); only the fleet control changes and distributes zone sets. | VDA5050_EN.md §6.4 and §6.4.1 zone table | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A115 | When a VDA 5050 robot loses its broker connection it keeps the order and drives to the last released node; the broker publishes a last-will message on `connection`. | VDA5050_EN.md §4.1 and §6.5 | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |
| A116 | VDA 5050 operating modes decide who controls the robot: in AUTOMATIC and SEMIAUTOMATIC fleet control sends orders; in INTERVENED only the cancelOrder instant action is allowed (orders are still allowed); in MANUAL, SERVICE and TEACH_IN fleet control sends neither orders nor instant actions. | VDA5050_EN.md §6.6.6 Table 10 and Table 11 | 2026-03 | primary | factory  |

| id | statement | source | date | reliability | tags |

| A301 | At Bosch Car Multimedia in Portugal, a five-megapixel camera images each metal spring of an automotive sensor from above, and the check runs in MVTec MERLIC with deep-learning anomaly detection. | https://www.mvtec.com/knowledge-base/news/article/an-eye-on-everything-with-deep-learning (whole article) | undated; proof of concept "end of 2022", new line "mid-2023" | secondary | factory  |
| A302 | Bosch's research project ViPAS (2019) used a robot to pick up the part and hold it in front of a camera inside a lighting dome, then classified it OK or NOK. | https://www.bosch-presse.de/pressportal/de/en/the-factory-of-the-future-bosch-is-turning-vision-into-reality-185920.html (ViPAS section) | 2019-03-27 | primary | factory  |
| A303 | Bosch's visual quality inspection (VQI) offering is a chain of blocks: image acquisition, image pre-processing, defect detection, PLC interface, then display and logging. | https://www.bosch-softwaretechnologies.com/media/images/products/innovation/aiandbigdata/Bosch_VQI_Whitepaper.pdf (PDF p.8 diagram, p.10 "Key components") | PDF created 2021-08-23 (no date printed) | primary | factory  |
| A304 | The VQI white paper says camera choice follows the minimum defect size, and lighting must be uniform with stray light kept out. | same PDF, p.17 "Environmental factors for deployment" | 2021 (PDF date) | primary | factory  |
| A305 | MVTec's Global Context Anomaly Detection uses two neural networks: a local one for small defects (scratches, cracks, dirt) and a global one for logical defects (bent or missing brackets); their outputs combine into one anomaly score. | MVTec article in A301 | undated (c. 2023) | secondary | factory  |
| A306 | The Bosch Car Multimedia model is trained on images of good parts only; no defect images are needed. | MVTec article in A301 | undated (c. 2023) | secondary | factory  |
| A307 | In that station the anomaly score is compared with a predefined threshold: above it the part is rejected, otherwise it passes. | MVTec article in A301 | undated (c. 2023) | secondary | factory  |
| A308 | The verdict reaches the machines over MQTT built into MERLIC because that plant has no PLC; each image can be reviewed in the front end with a heat map showing what triggered the alarm. | MVTec article in A301 | undated (c. 2023) | secondary | factory  |
| A309 | PatchCore, a standard anomaly-detection method, stores a "memory bank" of patch features from nominal (defect-free) images only and reports image-level AUROC of up to 99.6% on the MVTec AD benchmark. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.08265 (abstract) | v1 2021-06-15; v2 2022-05-05 | primary | general  |
| A310 | PatchCore's feature extractor is a network pre-trained on ImageNet (default WideResNet50, features from blocks 2 and 3) that is not adapted to the plant's images; PaDiM likewise uses a pre-trained CNN for patch embedding. | https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2106.08265 (section 3.1, appendix A); https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.08785 (abstract) | 2021-06 (v1); 2020-11-17 (PaDiM v1) | primary | supply-chain  |
| A311 | PatchCore's image-level anomaly score is the maximum distance between any test patch feature and its nearest neighbour in the memory bank (rescaled by neighbourhood distances). | https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2106.08265 (section 3.3, Eq. 6-7) | 2021-06 | primary | general  |
| A312 | The PatchCore paper chooses its decision threshold as the F1-optimal point on its test data and gives no general guidance for choosing a deployment threshold. | https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2106.08265 (appendix C.4, Table 1 caption) | 2021-06 | primary | general  |
| A313 | The open-source anomalib library (Intel/OpenVINO) sets one global F1-adaptive threshold during validation and returns a normalised score, a binary label, an anomaly map and a pixel mask. | https://anomalib.readthedocs.io/en/latest/markdown/guides/reference/post_processing/index.html (PostProcessor); https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.08341 (abstract) | docs read 2026-10-16; paper 2022-02-16 | primary | supply-chain  |
| A314 | anomalib issue #1028 (opened 2023-04-24 by samet-akcay, first author of the anomalib paper) says F1-adaptive thresholding needs anomalous samples in the validation set, which "might not always be available in real world anomaly detection problems"; the library's alternative is a fixed manual threshold (default 0.5). | https://github.com/openvinotoolkit/anomalib/issues/1028 (issue text); https://anomalib.readthedocs.io/en/v2.0.0/markdown/guides/reference/metrics/index.html (ManualThreshold) | issue opened 2023-04-24; docs v2.0.0 | primary | factory  |

| A401 | Szegedy et al. showed that a trained image-classification network can be made to misclassify an image by adding a perturbation that is imperceptible to people. | https://arxiv.org/abs/1312.6199 , abstract | 2013-12-21 (v1) | primary | general  |
| A402 | Goodfellow, Shlens and Szegedy attribute adversarial examples mainly to the near-linear behaviour of neural networks and give a one-step method (FGSM) to generate them. | https://arxiv.org/abs/1412.6572 , abstract | 2014-12-20 (v1) | primary | general  |
| A403 | Brown et al. built a printable "adversarial patch" that is universal (works on any scene) and targeted (forces a chosen class); in their physical demo a toaster-targeted sticker next to a banana flipped VGG16 from banana (97%) to toaster (99%). | https://arxiv.org/abs/1712.09665 , abstract; arXiv PDF Fig. 1 caption and the paragraph on the Demitasse app | 2017-12-27 (v1) | primary | general  |
| A404 | Eykholt et al. (CVPR 2018) put black-and-white stickers on a real stop sign: against their LISA-CNN classifier it was read as "Speed Limit 45" in 100% of lab images and in 84.8% of video frames from a moving car; against their second classifier, GTSRB-CNN, a sticker attack (target "Speed Limit 80") succeeded in 80% of stationary tests and 87.5% of extracted video frames. | https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.08945 , abstract and comments field ("Accepted to CVPR 2018"); PDF Sec. 1 and 4.3 | 2017-07-27 (v1) | primary | product  |

### B-attack (facts-B-attack.md, 55 rows)

| id | statement | source | date | reliability | tags |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| B1 | Eykholt et al. report that black-and-white stickers on a real stop sign made their own road-sign classifier output a chosen wrong class in 100% of lab images and in 84.8% of video frames filmed from a moving car. | https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.08945 (Abstract; Sec. 4.3 "Results for LISA-CNN", Table 3); PDF https://arxiv.org/pdf/1707.08945 | 2017-07-27 (v1); CVPR 2018 version v5 2018-04-10 | primary | product  |
| B2 | The Eykholt lab test used distances from 5 ft to 40 ft and the drive-by test started about 250 ft from the sign at 0 to 20 mph with a smartphone camera on a car. | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1707.08945 (Sec. 4.2 "Drive-By (Field) Tests"; Tables 1, 2, 5) | 2018-04-10 (v5) | primary | product  |
| B3 | Weaker Eykholt results: 66.67% for a graffiti-style sticker, 73.33% for a subtle right-turn-sign poster, and 80% (stationary) and 87.5% (drive-by) for a camouflage-art sticker against a second classifier (GTSRB-CNN). | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1707.08945 (Sec. 4.3 and 4.4; Tables 4 and 5) | 2018-04-10 (v5) | primary | product  |
| B4 | Cao et al. raised the success rate of making a LiDAR perception model see a fake obstacle about 5 m ahead from about 30% to around 75% by choosing which 60 spoofed points to inject. | https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.06826 (Abstract; Sec. 1; Sec. 3 threat model; Sec. 8.1 "Attack Effectiveness", Fig. 10, Table 4) | 2019-07-16 (arXiv); ACM CCS 2019 | primary | product  |
| B5 | In Cao et al.'s case study a spoofed obstacle made the simulated Apollo vehicle drop from 43 km/h to 0 km/h within 1 second, and the same trick held a car at a green light. | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1907.06826 (case-study section, Fig. 12 and Fig. 13) | 2019-08-20 (v2) | primary | product  |
| B6 | The Cao laser rig could reliably place about 60 fake LiDAR points, where a real vehicle returns about 2,000 points. | https://arxiv.org/pdf/1907.06826 (Sec. 6.2 "Number of spoofed points"); https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.16974 (Abstract) | 2019-08-20 (Cao v2); 2020-06-30 (Sun v1) | primary | product  |
| B7 | Sun et al. built a black-box LiDAR spoofing attack that reached about 80% mean success on all three common 3D-detector designs, and showed two defences that cut it to 5.5% and about 2.3%. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.16974 (Abstract) | 2020-06-30 (v1); USENIX Security 2020 | primary | product  |
| B8 | Nassi et al. made a Tesla Model X autopilot (HW 2.5 and HW 3) stop in the middle of a road for a phantom stop sign hidden in a digital-billboard-style advertisement; the Tesla reacted to a phantom shown for about 0.42 s (the authors' AutoSec 2021 demo used 500 ms, nine frames, on a 42-inch TV standing in for a billboard), while 125 ms is the duration reported to fool the Mobileye 630 PRO; the authors put the attack equipment at "a few hundred dollars". | https://www.nassiben.com/phantoms (TLDR and FAQ); paper: ACM CCS 2020 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3372297.3423359 (not readable, 403); abstract https://eprint.iacr.org/2020/085; AutoSec 2021 demo by the same author... | 2020 (ePrint received 2020-01-28; CCS Nov 2020) | primary (project page, AutoSec demo); secondary (WIRED repost) | product  |

### G2 (facts-G2.md, 31 rows)

| id | statement | source | date | reliability | tags |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| G101 | The AUTOSAR SecOC protocol specification says SecOC provides a mechanism to verify the authenticity and freshness of PDU-based communication between ECUs. | https://autosar.org/fileadmin/standards/R24-11/FO/AUTOSAR_FO_PRS_SecOcProtocol.pdf, sec. 1 p.5 and sec. 5.1 p.12; doc ID 969, AUTOSAR FO R24-11 | 2024-11-27 (R24-11 release); first release R20-11 = 2020-11-30 | primary | product  |
| G102 | The SecOC MAC is computed over the data identifier, the secured part of the Authentic I-PDU and the complete freshness value, and the Authentic I-PDU travels in clear inside the Secured I-PDU. | Same PRS, PRS_SecOc_00208 and PRS_SecOc_00211 p.17; definition of "Authentic I-PDU" sec. 4.2 p.10 | 2024-11-27 | primary |   |
| G103 | After the MAC and freshness check pass, the SecOC receiver passes the Authentic I-PDU to the upper layer, and none of the R24-11 SecOC documents (PRS, SWS, RS) contains a requirement to check whether the I-PDU content is plausible or correct. | PRS_SecOc_00320 PRS p.20 (and SWS_SecOC_00080 SWS p.57); absence found by keyword search of PRS (28 pp), SWS https://autosar.org/fileadmin/standards/R24-11/CP/AUTOSAR_CP_SWS_SecureOnboardCommunication.pdf (195 pp) and... | 2024-11-27 | primary (presence); absence = my search result | product  |
| G104 | SecOC has two configuration switches that make the receiver accept PDUs whose authentication failed; both default to false. | SWS R24-11 ECUC_SecOC_00052 (SecOCIgnoreVerificationResult) p.127; ECUC_SecOC_00051 (SecOCEnableForcedPassOverride) p.126; PRS_SecOc_00223 PRS p.19; PRS sec. 1.3.3 p.7 | 2024-11-27 | primary | product  |
| G105 | AUTOSAR SecOC Profile 3 ("JASPAR") uses AES-128 CMAC with a 64-bit freshness value of which 4 bits are sent, a MAC truncated to 28 bits, and is specified for CAN. | PRS R24-11 sec. 5.3.4 p.22 (PRS_SecOc_00630); the profile is in the CP SWS since release 4.3.0 https://www.autosar.org/fileadmin/standards/R4.3.0/CP/AUTOSAR_SWS_SecureOnboardCommunication.pdf, sec. 7.10.4 p.59 (SWS_Se... | R4.3.0 = 2016-11-30 (first SWS release with Profile 3); R4.3.1 = 2017-12-08 (per SWS R24-11 change history); R20-11 = 2020-11-30; text read in R24-11 (2024-11-27) | primary | product  |
| G106 | In the JASPAR freshness scheme a freshness-management master ECU synchronises trip and reset counters to all sender and receiver ECUs, and the spec says that when a counter reaches its maximum value replay attacks can no longer be detected. | SWS R24-11 Annex A.4, UC_SecOC_00202 p.171-172 (Table A.1, Table A.5 text); replay note p.175 | 2024-11-27 | primary | product  |
| G107 | SecOC can report only two security events to the AUTOSAR intrusion detection manager, MAC verification failed and a freshness-value failure (named SEV_SECOC_FRESHNESS_NOT_AVAILABLE in R24-11, renamed SEV_SECOC_FRESHNESS_NOT_OK in R25-11); both are marked DRAFT and reporting is off by default. | SWS R24-11 sec. 7.8, SWS_SecOC_00115 p.63; ECUC_SecOC_00114 p.126; RS R24-11 SRS_SecOC_00028 p.11 and SRS_SecOC_00017 p.16; R25-11 (current): SWS https://www.autosar.org/fileadmin/standards/R25-11/CP/AUTOSAR_CP_SWS_Se... | 2024-11-27 (R24-11); R25-11 = 2025-11-27 (current) | primary | product  |
